In the more expansive version, the product earns money for another round of development. Its owner can buy more software work without recruiting a corresponding number of employees, provided the systems and available resources support it. That could help Birch challenge a larger business. It could also help a well-funded incumbent advance faster than a challenger can assemble its first demonstration. The mechanism is a possible feedback between production and investment. Demand, competition, financing, and checking costs still determine its strength. A working agent team has not, by itself, demonstrated a firm that can finance and manage its own expansion.

The second possibility does not make the investment unproductive. An integrated service might become faster and more reliable. The question is whether a feasible way remains to test an alternative arrangement while preserving those benefits. Nor does the first possibility guarantee success. Birch's product could disappoint, its equipment could depreciate, and competitors could copy an improvement. Accumulation is a process with costs and rivals, not a promise that every early winner compounds without limit.

Anton Korinek and Donghyun Suh examine automation and capital accumulation together in their March 2024 scenario model. Investment can complement work that remains with people. Its pace relative to automation and the assumed range of human tasks can produce very different wage paths. Their model also separates reproducible capital, including compute, from fixed scarce factors. It gives no unconditional forecast that wages or monopoly profits must move in one direction.[^chapter_09-c9-capital]

For Birch, that distinction has a practical form. Another software agent cannot create a missing electricity connection or manufacture a server instantly. Nor does a temporary shortage establish permanent control. Investment could expand supply and invite competitors. To make the stronger gatekeeping argument, we would need to identify the enduring obstruction, what an outsider could do without it, and the costs of keeping another route available.

The ownership question concerns who holds claims on these productive assets. Suppose workers hold a stake in Birch. Suppose several small firms jointly finance equipment and evaluation. Suppose a public facility grants qualified teams access to computing. These are possible arrangements to investigate, not results already established by the workplace studies. Each changes claims on returns and authority over resources. Each still needs workable financing, competent operation, and a way to check whether its projects deserve more support.

Broad ownership would not automatically create independent discovery. A cooperative could be locked into one provider's records, while a single private owner could fund genuinely informative tests. Our junior's share certificate would answer who receives some income. It would not establish whether another model can be tried or an inconvenient failure reported. Distribution and the control of learning belong in the same conversation without becoming the same question.

Suppose the junior leaves to establish another reporting business. Under one hypothetical arrangement, the new firm can rent tools and build its own tests. Under another, reaching customers requires the old supplier's permission. Giving the worker a stake in the original employer would not necessarily create the missing route. A wider distribution of income and a wider distribution of entrepreneurial opportunity can still come apart.

Return to the first rung. A worker can be helped by cheaper instruction and harmed by losing an opportunity to demonstrate judgment. The firm employing that worker can be helped by affordable agents and constrained by the rights attached to them. The future of work therefore includes the future of the assets work builds: who can retain them, redirect them, and use them to attempt something new.

Those choices will be made under institutions as well as contracts. A country can encourage investment while permitting a common gate to close; it can also fund access through a procedure that becomes another gate. The next chapter follows that problem from Birch's workplace to the rules governing its opportunity to compete.

---

<a id="chapter_10"></a>

# Chapter 10

## Competition for future evidence

In January 2023, Britain's Competition and Markets Authority announced that six major banking providers had completed the remaining items in its Open Banking roadmap. This was an achievement with a qualification. Common standards had to remain usable. Performance and availability still needed monitoring. Completing implementation did not complete the obligation.[^chapter_10-c10-1]

Open Banking let customers share account information securely with authorized third parties and initiate payments through new services. Banks had to support common interfaces through which other businesses could actually use permitted information. An administrative right had to become a working connection.

Receiving your records in a form another service can interpret is more useful than an unexplained file. Being able to use that service reliably is another achievement again. A box marked data access can conceal a working route to an alternative or an interface that fails when needed.

The banks themselves had been required to create and fund the organization maintaining the standards. Completion left those funding duties in place. These were concrete obligations in a dated decision; their existence alone does not establish Open Banking's total economic benefit.[^chapter_10-c10-1]

Now put a different purchasing decision on the desk. A fictional council uses Aster to help sort building-maintenance requests. People describe leaking pipes, broken fittings, and other repairs. Software suggests a category and priority; a responsible human checks consequential decisions. Birch offers an alternative. The council must decide how to buy this work next year.

Aster has a record. Birch has demonstrations. Changing services could require staff training and data conversion. Keeping Aster might preserve a useful integrated system. It might also prevent the council from discovering whether Birch handles important requests better.

Would a feasible change in access or testing improve that purchase enough to justify its cost? This is the policy question I develop in an unpublished companion paper. The comparison budget helps identify a possible shortage of relevant opportunities. It does not tell us what those opportunities are worth.[^chapter_10-c10-2]

## What would the evidence change?

The council could renew, switch, divide the work, or redesign the service. Evidence has value when it helps choose among these actions more successfully. Suppose Birch classifies ordinary requests slightly better, but migration is expensive. A precise accuracy ranking might leave the best decision unchanged. A modest amount of evidence about missed urgent requests could matter much more.

David Blackwell supplied a foundational way to compare experiments. Under the classical conditions, if one experiment's observations can be produced from another's by adding a suitable kind of noise, the richer experiment can do at least as well across decision problems. Someone with the richer evidence can imitate the poorer procedure.[^chapter_10-c10-3]

Imagine a documented record of every eligible trial request: suggestion, checked outcome, and time required. A second report contains only a summary computed from those records. Someone with the interpretable full record can produce the summary; someone with the summary cannot generally recover discarded detail.

The classical argument does not establish that collecting this record is free, that publishing personal details is acceptable, or that every remedy improves welfare. Nor must experiments fit a universal ranking. A trial revealing performance on urgent repairs and another revealing integration costs answer different questions. Their relative usefulness depends on the purchase.

The council's first document should therefore describe its decision: eligible suppliers, work, period, and available actions. It should say how missed urgent requests, unnecessary escalations, delays, and costs count. Escalating every repair could produce an impressive safety statistic while wasting resources. Recording only missed emergencies would reward that behavior.

Residents' circumstances matter too. A delay one person can accommodate could substantially burden another. An average can conceal this choice of priorities. Aster might serve common requests better while Birch helps a smaller, consequential group. Buying both could be feasible. The experiment should permit an answer the purchaser can actually use.

The baseline must be feasible as well. If the council would renew Aster with existing human checks, compare the proposal with that arrangement. An imaginary alternative without checks would exaggerate its benefit; a perfectly informed buyer would set an unattainable benchmark.

An experiment can earn its keep while leaving the exact contributions of programmers, models, training sources, and earlier customers unresolved. Choosing a contract is less demanding than explaining every supplier's economic merit. Taking the inference limit seriously should direct institutions toward useful, attainable learning. It need not keep them experimenting until every question about contribution disappears.

## Opening the existing record

Before commissioning a trial, the fictional council should inspect existing evidence. Aster might have tested competing systems internally; supervisors or other purchasing units might have relevant checks. A large supplier can conduct excellent experiments. My theorem concerns a specified observed experiment, and all informative measurements belong inside it. Ignoring relevant private tests would manufacture a deficit.

The buyer still needs their protocol. Were both services eligible for comparable requests? Were hard cases withheld? Were these the versions now offered? Did supervisors know whose recommendations they checked? A detailed record can answer the wrong question.

Google's December 2025 search judgment makes this practical concern visible. Its specified data-sharing provisions required enough information for qualified competitors to understand the datasets, including descriptions of contents, sampling, and privacy-related transformations. It did not simply order delivery of an unexplained pile of records.[^chapter_10-c10-4]

This dated provision does not establish that implementation has succeeded. It highlights why interpretation can be part of access. A million query records and an explanation of how they were selected are different assets whose usefulness can depend on receiving both.

Suppose Aster's archive contains only suggestions staff accepted. A high success rate would describe those accepted suggestions, not automatically performance on rejected ones or requests never encountered. The audit should identify what is missing. It could also find convincing evidence that Aster is more reliable and cheaper on the actual work. An informational argument must be capable of recommending no additional pilot.

Zahra Meghani's prior work on epistemic monopoly warns about dependence on a developer for evaluation evidence and the power to set research conditions. I borrow that concern while asking who controls comparisons relevant to a specified economic decision.[^chapter_10-c10-5] Dishonesty is unnecessary: Aster can accurately report its chosen investigations while leaving the buyer's decisive question unanswered.

Scrutiny requires expertise, secure handling, and time. A remedy should request the fields and checks needed for the decision. An authorized evaluator could inspect private requests and report methods, uncertainty, and conclusions. Public accountability need not expose residents' addresses. Credibility depends on what can be checked and challenged.

Disclosure can also change competition. The 2023 Merger Guidelines discuss how easier observation of rivals' terms can increase susceptibility to coordination. Giving every competitor every business record need not improve the strategic environment. An independent evaluator and a rival sales department have different uses for confidential information.

Open Banking's completion decision distinguished service-quality indicators and underlying data from current-account transaction records supporting authorized account access and payments. It specified particular services, rather than ordering publication of every internal file.[^chapter_10-c10-6] Our council needs that specificity: residents can receive reasons for the purchase, evaluators can inspect relevant requests, and suppliers can challenge claims. Confidentiality should protect people while permitting scrutiny of the assignment and checking procedures.

## A file that can travel

Suppose residents' earlier requests, staff corrections, and categories sit in a format Birch cannot use. Switching would require rebuilding that history. Portability might lower the barrier. Open Banking's common standards, conformance checks, and continuing performance obligations provide a real reference: compatibility was a maintained service.[^chapter_10-c10-6]

Ipsos's February 2026 publication supplies another kind of banking evidence. Its independent service-quality survey, conducted during 2025 under a regulatory requirement, asked customers about recommending providers and particular services, including online banking and branches.[^chapter_10-c10-13] These experiences matter, but customers were not randomly assigned to banks. Their needs can differ.

A resident's response after a repair would likewise describe an experience without isolating the respective contributions of software, maintenance crew, and easy assignment. Different observations can improve a decision without becoming interchangeable kinds of evidence.

A portable history could help Birch distinguish a new leak from a follow-up and save staff from re-entering information. These are direct operating benefits. But copying that history to three suppliers does not produce three independent observations of what would have happened had each served the council from the beginning.

Interpretation matters too. Imagine a field marked positive means successfully resolved in one record version, but problem requiring action in another. Exported files omit the version. A reader could reverse the results while processing every row correctly. Under one interpretation, following the signal helps; under the other, it hurts.

This deliberately stark example comes from my policy draft. Knowing that someone with the correct interpretation could use the records does not supply the actual buyer with a usable procedure. If the records cannot distinguish the meanings, more equally ambiguous rows may not help. A verified definition can let the buyer use one procedure that reads the version and interprets the result.

Definitions alone reveal no outcomes. Outcomes with unknown meanings may be unusable. Together they can support a decision; their joint value need not equal the sum of their separate values. Freely available, understood evidence need not harm a buyer who can ignore it. Cost and lost interpretation create the problem. Retaining the trusted record preserves the earlier procedure.

What survives migration should therefore be specified: category definitions, corrections, and the link between a result and its service version. Counting exported fields is an inadequate test of usefulness.

Interoperability asks whether Birch can interact with the systems and users it needs. A narrow interface receiving eligible requests and returning recommendations might suffice. Access to every internal component might be unnecessary. Birch must maintain its connection and protect records; Aster can incur costs when interfaces change. Disputes and responsibility for those burdens need procedures too.

Compatibility can bring security, capacity, and maintenance costs and alter investment incentives. The appraisal must retain these consequences and the benefits of integration. An entrant's need does not establish that someone else should bear every cost it requests.

## Making the missing comparison

An audit cannot recover an unobserved outcome. Portability need not show how Birch performs on fresh work. If the missing contrast matters enough, the fictional council needs a pilot. Qualified services would receive comparable requests under a documented rule, with human checks and records of errors, time, versions, and missing results.

Requests can interact. Unnecessary escalations from one service could fill the shared maintenance queue and delay the other's repairs. The buyer might compare periods or teams, or account for the queue explicitly. Each solution changes the test and its cost. Balanced assignment alone cannot make an operating comparison interpretable.

On June 3, 2019, a field experiment began calling rice farmers in India. Precision Agriculture for Development wanted to choose how to recruit farmers to its telephone service. Maximilian Kasy and Anja Sautmann helped compare six combinations: morning or evening automated calls, with no text alert, an alert an hour ahead, or one a day ahead. From a government partner's list, the service set aside ten thousand valid numbers outside the do-not-disturb register. Seventeen waves of calls followed, with assignment changing as results arrived.[^chapter_10-c10-7]

The morning call preceded by an hour's warning received nearly four thousand assignments. The evening call without a warning received fewer than four hundred. Every option was tried, but the assignments were unequal. At the end, the authors put the probability that the morning call with that warning was best at just over three quarters, under their statistical model. Uncertainty remained.[^chapter_10-c10-7]

Success meant answering five recruitment questions, not a better harvest or higher income. The calls could guide the next campaign without establishing the agricultural service's total value. The council similarly needs evidence fitted to its available decision.

It could adapt assignments as results arrive, giving a promising service more requests while preserving informative alternatives. Serving today's requests and learning what to buy next year need not recommend identical allocations.

The comparison budget accumulates modeled allocation chance outside the current favorite. It prescribes neither equal shares nor a universal quota. A small persistent chance can accumulate indefinitely; a large chance can be wasted on indistinguishable tasks. Sending Birch easy broken-handle cases will not resolve an uncertain difference on ambiguous water damage.

The buyer needs a declared rule for using results, such as adopting Birch for specified work if improvement justifies migration. Otherwise, a disappointment can be relabeled as success by changing the criterion afterward.

An impressive pilot can travel badly. It might contain unusually clear requests or receive unusually experienced staff. Versions can change before renewal. My policy draft retains several plausible accounts of how trial performance transfers to operation, then asks whether one feasible procedure works adequately across them. The council could broaden the test, improve calibration, narrow the contract, or decline to switch.

Such assurance concerns expected performance across the retained accounts. It cannot promise every resident a better outcome or every realized contract a gain. Investigators should examine credible evidence and justified assumptions as well as what subsequently happened. Fortune alone cannot validate a defective method, and misfortune need not condemn a reasonable choice.

An analyst secretly told the true explanation could choose differently in each imagined world. A council receives no such document. A useful proposal must specify a procedure it can follow with the evidence actually available.

## When every road leads to the same judge

Our fictional councils could each buy from a different website and still receive one supplier ranking. They could commission separate trials using the same defective checklist. Adding buyers would then multiply encounters with a common judgment. An independent route matters when it changes the relevant selection or measurement process, not merely the name on the invoice.

This is the historical possibility running from Smith's towns through Bell's network to platform work. Wider markets can support more specialization, better service, and more customers. They can also extend one institution's reach over the decisions determining who may develop. A market can grow while the number of independent places to try again shrinks. That is a proposition about institutions to investigate, not a measured law of history.

Consider an explicit hypothetical. Suppose OpenAI became the only foundational AI supplier. Add a further condition: it also controlled indispensable routes to customers, the evaluations deciding admission, and access needed to develop competing systems. These are assumptions about a possible arrangement, not a description of OpenAI today or a forecast. The question concerns the structure, whichever company might occupy it.

Even a sole model supplier would not automatically possess total epistemic monopoly. Independent laboratories could retain ground truth, buyers could test alternatives, and other technologies could answer the question at issue. The stronger scenario requires control over the production and checking of relevant comparisons. Its permission becomes necessary to test the alternatives to its authority, with no workable independent route remaining.

The service might be excellent and cheap. Its owners might have invested wisely and lawfully. Those facts belong in the appraisal. But a satisfied present customer cannot report the performance of a rival that never received a feasible development route. The concern reaches beyond a price increase: which alternative capabilities will be permitted to become observable?

Saying that markets clear does not answer this. Clearing concerns offers that can be made under existing conditions. It does not guarantee that an undeveloped supplier acquires the inputs, customers, or evidence needed to make a competitive offer. Nor do a million agents necessarily provide a million independent judgments if access to all of them passes through the same gate.

Calling the scenario science fiction is not an institutional safeguard. Neither is describing it enough to justify intervention. The serious question is whether an identifiable arrangement could make independent evaluation prohibitively difficult, and whether reversing it later would require rebuilding vanished skills, infrastructure, or organizations. That inquiry can begin before one supplier controls everything.

Hayek's competition as discovery already tells us why unfamiliar possibilities matter. My narrower contribution concerns what a specified allocation history can reveal about productive effects.[^chapter_10-c10-9] The comparison-budget theorem does not prove a centuries-long transition, measure the AI market, or establish that a platform has a finite lifetime comparison budget. It gives us a precise object; the historical and policy extensions need their own evidence.

Bell's experience shows why the route has stages. Opening patents supported follow-on innovation outside telecommunications, while the study found a different result within the sector. Permission to use knowledge was not the entire route to a customer.[^chapter_10-c10-8] Publishing an incumbent's records and allowing an entrant to generate new records can therefore accomplish different things. A thousand copies of one history remain copies of one history.

Scale also supplies research, reliability, and lower costs. One large supplier can conduct informative internal tests; several firms can repeat a shared error. Private testing counts. A public trial can displace it. The aim is to preserve consequential independent learning where its benefits justify its full costs, rather than keep every proposed competitor alive.

## Precaution without closing discovery

Competition policy already reaches beyond prices. The United States' 2023 Merger Guidelines discuss products, features, innovation, wages, benefits, and research competition. The proposed information rationale adds a question about future evidence; it does not discover nonprice antitrust or dispense with statutory requirements.[^chapter_10-c10-10]

Here is the policy position I favor. When a gatekeeper controls an indispensable route to useful independent evaluation, and there is credible evidence that closing it would be hard to reverse, the presumption should favor keeping a workable route open. Waiting for the last effective challenger to disappear can destroy the evidence needed to appraise the decision. The burden should then fall on the proposed restriction to demonstrate why a narrower arrangement cannot protect legitimate interests.

That is a precautionary rule with a defined task: preserve a valuable, feasible option to learn before it becomes prohibitively costly to recover. It is not a promise that every option deserves preservation. Arrow and Anthony Fisher's work on uncertainty and irreversibility provides an antecedent. When experience can inform a later choice, acting irreversibly now can sacrifice that option. They explicitly retained cost-benefit appraisal; irreversibility alone does not forbid investment.[^chapter_10-c10-precaution]

The rule could require documented interfaces, workable interoperability, voluntary data portability, or access to specified knowledge needed for a credible test. Requirements should follow the obstruction. Search data access cannot supply a missing electricity connection; model access cannot repair a dishonest outcome measure. A regulator should identify what the entrant could actually do under the proposed terms.

The EU's Digital Markets Act gives this boundary a concrete form. For designated messaging services, gatekeepers must connect specified functions they already offer their own users: individual messages and attachments first, group messaging within two years of designation, voice and video calls within four. Access is upon request and free of charge. The law also requires preservation of the security provided to the service's own users, including end-to-end encryption where applicable.[^chapter_10-c10-dma]

That is an obligation to build and maintain connections between privately owned services. A separate provision concerns access to specified search data on fair, reasonable and nondiscriminatory terms. The two duties have different designs. They demonstrate that ownership can coexist with participation rights, without establishing that every implementation succeeds or that an AI company automatically owes the same duty.[^chapter_10-c10-dma]

For AI, an obligation might concern an interface or a secure evaluation route rather than publication of every model weight or training record. Fees may be justified; privacy, security, research incentives, and genuine technical constraints matter. Claims of unavoidable harm should receive independent scrutiny. Letting the gatekeeper alone decide whether a competing test is safe would make the safeguard depend on the permission it is intended to examine.

The public authority also needs limits. It should specify the affected decision, plausible lost comparison, legal power, expected benefit, costs, and less burdensome substitutes. A secure audit may suffice where existing evidence answers the question. Prospective trials matter when no file can contain the missing contrast. My proposed time-limited evaluation power would require legislation wherever existing authority is insufficient, along with challenge and review.[^chapter_10-c10-11]

These duties preserve private ownership while limiting control over other people's routes to discovery. The owner can retain a return on invention without receiving an unlimited right to decide which alternatives may become knowable. That is a normative commitment, supported where the additional causal and welfare case holds. It is not a theorem about what property must mean.

Government can also become the single judge. A mandatory benchmark, favored consultant, or official platform could synchronize mistakes across an economy. Independent appeal, alternative methods, and revision must constrain the evaluator as well as the evaluated. Precaution should keep discovery possible on both sides of the public-private boundary.

## Who can keep the next entrant possible?

Follow Birch back to the months before its first demonstration. In this thought experiment, agents help the small team write software and investigate failures. But the service still needs computing, permission to use relevant records, a customer willing to try it, and a credible way to check the results. The workers could own every share in the company and still need another organization's permission to take that next step.

Three documented arrangements put that problem into focus: a European computing application, an American research initiative, and a Chinese industrial policy. Follow what each could enable a qualifying team to build, keep, and test. The records establish particular terms and objectives. Birch supplies the explicitly hypothetical business through which we examine their possible consequences.

EuroHPC's Playground call, opened in April 2025, offers an entry route for European startups and smaller businesses with limited computing needs. Applications arrive in order, with eligibility and technical checks rather than a competitive ranking. Allocations last one, two, or three months on a single system. The published call also warns that demand can leave particular capacity unavailable. The door has a procedure and a queue.[^chapter_10-c10-europe]

Its Industrial Innovation modes offer eligible AI small businesses free computing for innovation. Crucially, the March 2026 terms say generated data and models remain the user's property and can be commercially exploited. They also require publication of results outside confidential portions and a final report. Public provision and private accumulation coexist in these stated terms.[^chapter_10-c10-europe]

For a qualifying version of Birch, this could mean building an asset before it can afford its own cluster. The public facility supplies a productive input; the firm can retain something it develops. But that is only the first stage. Computing time does not oblige a council to buy, make unsuitable training data useful, or establish that the service handles urgent repairs well. Those remain separate problems.

Suppose Birch reaches the end of its allocation with a prototype. Its next customer asks how it handles an urgent repair described ambiguously. Can the team reproduce its test, explain the mistakes, and run a revised version elsewhere? Those capabilities would make the public input useful beyond the allocation itself. Count the waiting and support costs too, and ask whether Birch would otherwise have bought equivalent computing. A busy facility can subsidize existing plans as well as make new experiments possible.

This arrangement does not require Europe to abandon property. Article 17 of the EU Charter protects possessions and intellectual property while allowing regulation of use under specified conditions. Its text supplies neither an automatic license to compel access nor a comparative enforcement score.[^chapter_10-c10-property] The question is which particular rights and duties sustain investment and entry together. Nor is the EU one government: common institutions and member states must still make different parts of an arrangement work.

America provides a useful challenge to the claim that commitment to private enterprise leaves a state unable to act. The July 2025 AI Action Plan advocates deregulation, but also encourages open models and wider access to computing. It identifies long commitments to large cloud suppliers as an obstacle for many startups and academics, and recommends developing computing markets and the National AI Research Resource, or NAIRR. These are policy recommendations, not proof that the obstacle has disappeared.[^chapter_10-c10-america]

In September 2026, the National Science Foundation announced an operations center for NAIRR, the public-private research initiative. The center would coordinate providers, integrate computing and other resources, operate a portal, and support users. A researcher could potentially reach resources through an organized public route rather than negotiate every component alone. The announcement establishes an institutional undertaking; it does not measure how many otherwise impossible competitors it creates.[^chapter_10-c10-nairr]

An American version of Birch would still need an appropriate commercial route; a research program is not a general business entitlement. The portal also need not remove dependence on its participating providers. Imagine a university collaborator can test a design, but the firm cannot carry the resulting checks into its next customer trial. Research access would have helped, while the business still faced a missing connection. Eligibility, permitted use, and transferable results determine how far the route actually reaches.

American authorities have also examined the private arrangements. In January 2025, FTC staff reported on partnerships between major cloud suppliers and AI developers. The report identified potential concerns involving input access, contractual and technical switching costs, and privileged information. It explicitly made no determination of illegality. Its historical scope matters: these were findings from a dated inquiry, not an audit of every contract now available.[^chapter_10-c10-ftc]

Such partnerships can finance capabilities that would otherwise remain undeveloped. Breaking a productive relationship can destroy value. But financing can also come with obligations that shape where the recipient can go next. To evaluate Birch's position, inspect the agreement and the feasible substitutes. Saying America lacks any tools is untenable. Whether it uses sufficient tools, with the right incentives and competence, remains a question about institutions in operation.

The conditional risk is that a government protects a leading private system so thoroughly that other private businesses must develop on its terms. Removing an unnecessary permit could help both Aster and Birch. Leaving an indispensable interface entirely at Aster's discretion could have a different effect. Counting the regulations removed tells us little about which firm can conduct the next credible test. A government can reduce bureaucracy and still need a carefully specified access duty.

China's August 2025 AI Plus policy starts from a more explicit program of coordination. It calls for shared and interconnected computing, agent services, open-source development, and patient capital. It addresses new forms of work and employment risks as well as industrial adoption. These are announced objectives. They do not establish that an unaffiliated firm actually receives resources or that the policy improves long-run adaptation.[^chapter_10-c10-china]

For a Chinese version of our hypothetical team, longer funding could pay for another redesign before the first customer arrives. Shared computing could make that redesign affordable. But imagine that the facility's admission test rewards the established supplier's approach and rejects Birch's different way of classifying requests before a customer can try it. Coordinated resources would then coexist with a common gate. The policy's benefits depend on what teams can actually attempt, including approaches its administrators did not anticipate.

There is a further boundary. China's 2023 interim generative-AI measures encourage innovation and shared infrastructure, while imposing political content requirements on covered services offered to the mainland public. Their scope expressly excludes development and applications that do not provide such public services. That distinction prevents us from treating every private research activity as governed by the same provision. It also prevents us from treating support for computing as equivalent to unrestricted public inquiry.[^chapter_10-c10-china-rules]

An economy could make it easier to try competing industrial designs while restricting which politically unwelcome conclusions a public service may generate. These are different dimensions of discovery. For the book's institutional proposal, independent checking must include the possibility that a commercially or officially favored system fails. Public support for experiments loses part of its value when the resulting evidence cannot be used to challenge the sponsor's preferred conclusion.

An excluded applicant's rights and the ability to report failure therefore belong in the comparison alongside computing and finance. Europe and China cannot be grouped into one alternative to America simply because both may intervene. Nor does a larger public budget settle the question. Follow the attempted product, its tests, and the grounds on which somebody can refuse the next step.

Here is the possibility worth taking seriously: limiting a gatekeeper's exclusion rights could preserve more room for private enterprise than leaving that bottleneck untouched. Imagine Birch receives a narrow, secure connection and wins customers through a better service. It remains private; Aster remains private. The state has changed the terms under which they can compete. That is a specific institutional choice to appraise, not a promise that intervention always enlarges opportunity.

The counterexample should be equally concrete. Suppose obtaining the connection requires an expensive certification only Aster can afford. Or suppose publication depends on an official evaluator who suppresses inconvenient failures. Birch then faces a new gate. A rule written to preserve discovery can make the market harder to enter. Reliable limits on public power matter to private accumulation too: investors need grounds to expect that a successful experiment will leave them something worth owning.

To choose between arrangements, return to the same sequence. Can a qualified outsider obtain resources? Can it reach customers and receive informative work? Can someone check the outcomes independently? Can useful records travel? Can an exclusion or a failed official test be challenged? The answers must survive ordinary use. A published entitlement that nobody can exercise is the institutional equivalent of the exported file nobody can read.

The society with the first leading model need not be the society best able to recover from a mistake in it. Conversely, a generous access program might absorb scarce engineers and money while producing little useful knowledge. The long-run hypothesis is about preserving workable alternatives at acceptable cost. We will return to how to test it in the final chapter. These dated policies open that inquiry; they do not settle which society will prevail.

## The price of finding out

The fictional council's menu now includes an audit, portable records, a narrow interface, a pilot, independent purchasing routes, and no additional experiment. There is no permanent ranking. An audit wins when useful evidence already exists. An archive loses when the decisive outcome was never observed. Replication can be worth its cost when shared mistakes dominate the decision.

The appraisal can be ordinary. What consequential mistake could the procedure prevent, over what period? How likely is it to help? What will testing, migration, supervision, privacy protection, and lost production cost? Is there a cheaper substitute? The hypothetical calculations in my policy draft specify these inputs; a real purchase would need evidence for them.[^chapter_10-c10-12]

Judgments about rare serious errors, residents' different burdens, and future benefits must be visible. More data cannot decide an undeclared value judgment. Costs also need honest bookkeeping. Lost operating output should not be counted twice under different headings. A payment between participants is not automatically a social resource loss; a burden outside the purchasing department is not automatically irrelevant.

Suppose supervisors check Birch's recommendations. Their work can improve today's repairs and future evidence. Count both benefits without duplicating the same improvement. Stop when remaining differences would no longer change the contract, attainable information costs too much, or the trial cannot answer the intended question. Continuing indefinitely would consume resources needed for repairs.

The theorem's failure of universal contribution recovery can coexist with adequate choice among a finite menu over a stated horizon. The council need not resolve every attribution to make a worthwhile purchase.

The public-interest case needs another step. If the council captures all benefits and can contract for evidence, it might solve the problem itself. A trial benefiting other buyers or workers could leave private incentives short of social value. Investigate that gap. Concentration alone cannot establish it.

Birch might want an expensive trial that chiefly subsidizes sales; Aster might prefer an audit too narrow to challenge its position. Require both to explain how their proposal improves the purchase and at whose expense. Residents need repairs handled well. Impressive competition statistics cannot compensate for a remedy that degrades their service.

Open Banking's continuing obligations supply the final image: a route other services could use, maintained after implementation. For the council, the achievement is an actual opportunity to compare qualified alternatives and make a better purchase. A formal opening begins that work.

The next chapter asks whether taxation could address conduct that removes useful comparison and leaves costs outside the actor's account. Before discussing a rate, we need to identify the behavior, the affected decision, and evidence for the claimed social cost.

---

<a id="chapter_11"></a>

# Chapter 11

## Taxing lost comparison

Imagine the fictional council from the previous chapter receiving an invoice for a new supplier trial. Staff must prepare requests, check outcomes, supervise the work, and preserve an interpretable record. The test will cost money. It is needed because a separate purchasing route through which Birch might have demonstrated its service has disappeared.

Suppose, for this thought experiment, that an access condition chosen by Aster removed that route. The condition also made the council's existing service cheaper to operate. There is a productive benefit and a possible informational cost. Charging Aster because it is successful would fail to distinguish them. Ignoring the lost test because the service became cheaper would miss the other side of the choice.

The invoice gives the problem a useful shape. What evidence was lost? What decision would it have helped? Can the council obtain an adequate substitute? Who benefits from that substitute, and who would otherwise pay for it? These questions come before a tax rate.

My tax extension starts at this point. It asks whether a specified action removes useful comparison and creates a cost outside the decision maker's private account. It also asks what happens when firms buy their own tests and governments fund replacements. The answers require economic assumptions beyond the comparison-budget theorem.[^chapter_11-c11-1]

The historical argument changes the possible size of that missing account. If another town offers a genuinely separate trial, closing one local route may leave a feasible substitute. If a shared gate controls the routes across many towns, replacement may require rebuilding an institution rather than purchasing another test. These are conditional comparisons, not estimates. They explain why a rule designed for local exclusion may be inadequate when a restriction travels across a network. Property rights need not settle who bears that additional cost. The owner can retain the service and its productive returns while facing an obligation to keep a testing route open or contribute to a justified replacement. Whether a charge can do that better than an access rule is a question for appraisal, not an assumption that taxation creates the lost history.

Aster's income raises two questions. How much reflects its engineers' work, and how much reflects the position it acquired? And if its conduct prevents valuable comparisons, should it bear some of the resulting cost? Explaining an existing fortune and changing the incentives governing future conduct are different tasks.

## The income and its explanation

Aster has delivered a service and received payment. Its engineering hours, expenditure, and product characteristics can be recorded. These observations establish things the company did. They do not necessarily establish exactly how much those inputs changed its chance of receiving the contract.

An engineer's recorded hours illustrate the distinction. In our fictional Aster, payroll records could establish that the engineer worked, and a technical check could establish that the delivered program meets a specification. Those are meaningful facts. A question about how much this work changed the probability of winning against Birch needs another comparison. A further question about the program's benefit to residents needs an outcome measure. The same bundle of records may inform several questions, but it does not collapse them into one. Paying the engineer under an agreed contract need not await a perfect answer to every attribution question. Nor does keeping the payment record turn it into the missing experiment.

Consider a simplified version of the supplier example in which recorded input profiles stay fixed. Winning chances depend on both the effect of those inputs and an unobserved positional component. One explanation gives the inputs a larger effect and assigns less advantage to position. Another gives the inputs a smaller effect and assigns more advantage to position. Under the specified adjustment, the two components can offset each other.[^chapter_11-c11-2]

The important statement is stronger than saying that two stories fit one invoice. In this constructed setting, the admissible explanations can give the same probabilities to every observable allocation history. Watching the record grow cannot distinguish them through that experiment. Yet they can assign different values to a defined contribution question about what changing an input would do.

The tax authority receives the same observable evidence under both explanations. A rule using only that evidence cannot promise one exact contribution division in the first economy and a different exact division in the second. It has no observation on which to base the distinction. Calling the rule sophisticated does not supply the missing information.

These are different candidate economic explanations, with different input effects and hidden position. They are not two shadow futures in which the underlying effects remain fixed and only the history changes. Nor is this the finite-lifetime-budget result from the earlier chapters. It is a separate observational-equivalence argument, with its own conditions.

The fixed-input condition matters. If Aster changes relevant inputs over time in a way that the same hidden positional component cannot absorb, informative variation may break the equivalence. Additional measurements can help too. The example does not establish that all productive effects are unknowable or that every possible tax base is uninformative.

For the council, independently measured repair outcomes could reveal things missing from an award record. A test might establish that a service catches a particular kind of urgent request. That observation belongs in the evidence. We cannot discard it simply because doing so makes an attribution limit easier to state.

Even a carefully estimated effect would answer a specified question. How did changing an input affect expected rewards over a stated period? How did a service affect repair quality? Those are different targets. Neither automatically supplies the moral value of the owners' income. A causal calculation and a judgment about what someone deserves remain distinct operations.

The practical conclusion is limited but consequential. If a tax proposal promises to identify the precisely earned fraction of every realized reward from the market record alone, it may be demanding something the record cannot deliver. Taxation can instead use observable bases, ranges of plausible effects, and principles that do not require this exact retrospective decomposition.

That is already familiar territory in public finance. Thomas Piketty, Emmanuel Saez, and Stefanie Stantcheva distinguish responses involving productive activity, tax avoidance, and compensation bargaining. These margins have different implications for policy. Their framework does not wait for an exact causal-merit certificate to be attached to each taxpayer's income.[^chapter_11-c11-3]

Casey Rothschild and Florian Scheuer likewise study taxation when income can combine productive and rent-seeking activities that the authority cannot separately observe for each person. The response to taxation depends on how returns across activities change. Their analysis warns against assuming that a simple average of unobserved components produces the right corrective rate.[^chapter_11-c11-4]

These predecessors already model hidden activities and behavioral responses. My result addresses a narrower demand: an exact retrospective division of rewards based on a specified market record. It asks whether that record can distinguish the candidate explanations, before anyone promises a tax authority that it can.

My own commitments remain those stated earlier: useful work and valuable experimentation should have substantial rewards; people should have enough security to survive failure; capable alternatives should have practical routes to demonstrate themselves. Those are positions to defend openly. They are not percentages that fall out of an impossibility theorem.

For Birch's workers, income support after losing a contract might provide security. A funded test might provide a chance to demonstrate the service. For Aster, predictable contractual payment might support investment. These instruments pursue different aims and can have different costs. We should examine their effects without pretending that a complete ledger of moral merit has already settled the choice.

Return to Birch's agent workforce. Suppose the workers own shares in the company that keeps the code and equipment. A profitable year could bring wages and dividends. Broader ownership changes who receives the returns; an income tax addresses receipts under its own specified rules. Neither measures lost comparison. Widely distributed profits could come from an arrangement that blocks an independent test. Concentrated profits could finance excellent experiments.

Now suppose a narrow access duty lets a departing worker establish another firm and test a different service. That preserves an entrepreneurial route. A corrective charge addresses another question: does particular conduct impose a demonstrated cost on others that the firm does not bear? These instruments can complement each other, but the share certificate, tax return, and access contract answer different questions. An inherited stake is not a meter of information destruction. An agent invoice is not one either. The comparative argument gives governments choices to investigate; it does not turn private success into a calibrated tax bill.

## A charge needs a base

Sweden's nitrogen-oxide charge supplies a concrete contrast. Introduced in 1992, the system charged covered energy-production facilities for emissions and returned receipts in relation to useful energy output. The design sought to reward low emissions relative to production. It distinguished a harmful by-product from the useful activity that produced it.[^chapter_11-c11-5]

The charge also depended on measurement. The Swedish environmental agency describes requirements for emissions instruments, calculations, declarations, audits, and checks against independent measurements. Whatever judgment one makes about the complete policy, its administrators need to know what the charged quantity means. A meter, a definition, and a verification procedure are part of the instrument.

Under the agency's guidance, a facility's measuring equipment must be checked at least annually against independent measurements from an accredited laboratory. The agency also coordinates comparisons that check the laboratories themselves. There is a chain of verification behind the declared quantity.[^chapter_11-c11-5]

Our council needs a different chain: eligible alternatives, relevant tasks, checked results, and retained failures. A receipt for a million tests would establish activity. It would leave open whether those tests distinguished the alternatives. That is why preserving the selection rule matters alongside preserving the test results.

Our council has a harder measurement problem. It cannot attach a sensor to Aster's contract and read out units of destroyed knowledge. A lost supplier assignment might have been informative, redundant, or too poorly measured to help. Its value depends on the decision and on what other evidence remains available.

This is why the comparison budget cannot serve as a monetary tax bill. It accumulates modeled allocation chances outside the current favorite. A cost to the public requires a further account of how an action changes those opportunities, what observations they would produce, and how their absence affects a consequential choice. The object diagnoses a feature of an experiment; it does not price the damage.

Suppose Aster's access condition removes Birch's trial on routine requests that both suppliers already handle equally well. Losing that contrast might have little decision value. Suppose instead it prevents the only feasible test of an important failure. The loss could be more consequential. The same number of removed assignments would conceal the difference.

The Swedish example also helps separate a payment's incentive from what happens to its receipts. Returning money according to useful output does not make the emissions charge disappear. It changes the overall incentives and distribution. Likewise, earmarking an informational levy for testing would not establish that the levy targeted the right harmful action, or that the resulting tests were worth buying.

A corrective argument must begin with a causal change. Would altering the access condition actually restore the relevant opportunity? Perhaps Birch would not participate, even with access. Perhaps the council lacks staff to supervise the test. Perhaps another unit already runs an adequate comparison. An association between scale and fewer rival observations would not settle these alternatives.

The argument then needs beneficiaries. If better evidence helps Aster's own next purchase and Aster captures the benefit, it already has a reason to investigate. If it also improves decisions for other buyers whose benefits Aster cannot collect, part of the value lies outside its account. That difference can create a reason for correction, provided the remaining conditions hold.

Kenneth Arrow's 1962 analysis supplies a foundation. Knowledge can arise through deliberate research and as a by-product of ordinary economic activity. Its production, use, and appropriation create problems that a frictionless market benchmark does not resolve automatically. Information economics already gives us reasons to examine the gap between private rewards and wider benefits.[^chapter_11-c11-6]

The new question is about a particular route through which useful evidence ceases to be produced. Aster's condition could affect that route while improving current service. The appraisal should retain both effects. A charge that discouraged valuable integration more than it improved later decisions would fail on its own economic terms.

It should also retain legitimate privacy and security costs. A missing public record is not automatically a record society should require someone to publish. The relevant alternative might be secure independent evaluation, rather than unrestricted access. The cost of providing usable evidence includes the safeguards needed to make that alternative acceptable.

## Two choices inside one firm

The unpublished tax draft lets the firm make two choices. It chooses an activity that has productive returns but reduces an existing stream of informative trials. It also chooses how much private testing to buy. The second choice is essential. Without it, an analysis can mistake lost routine feedback for an inevitable loss of total knowledge.[^chapter_11-c11-7]

Imagine Aster receiving less useful feedback from ordinary operations as its service becomes more integrated. It could commission tests to fill part of the gap. The company has a reason to do so if better evidence improves a decision from which it benefits. The private experiment is real evidence, even if it takes place inside a large incumbent.

Private replacement can also change the appropriate activity charge. Suppose each lost ordinary test leads Aster to purchase an equivalent test, within the draft's specified response setting. More integration then increases its testing bill while leaving the relevant total evidence unchanged. Charging it again as though the decision maker had lost that same information would ignore what the firm already paid to restore. The remaining problem could be that the privately chosen total is too low from the wider beneficiaries' perspective. Correcting that testing target is different from charging for a marginal evidence loss that substitution has prevented. This does not make private evaluation a blanket exemption. It means the fiscal calculation must follow the actual response. When private replacement stops, or cannot supply the same information, the effect of another lost organic test can change. A formula derived while holding testing fixed cannot simply be applied after testing adjusts.

The draft uses a deliberately tractable experiment. Informative measurements help choose between two alternatives. Organic observations, privately purchased tests, and public tests supply comparable evidence, with errors independent given the underlying state. Results from all three sources reach the same decision maker. The decision has a stated payoff, and tests have stated costs. Those assumptions make the substitution problem possible to analyze precisely.

They are not descriptions we can assume for every AI evaluation. A supplier's internal test and a public field experiment may reveal different dimensions. One may use synthetic tasks while the other observes actual users. Their errors may be correlated. Before applying the draft's conclusions, an investigator must establish which observations can adequately substitute for which others.

Within its stated setting, the firm can value information less than society does because it receives only part of the benefit. It may therefore buy fewer tests than would be worthwhile for the beneficiaries taken together. A charge on the activity that removes organic trials can influence that activity while leaving the incentive to buy tests insufficiently corrected.

That is the draft's two-choice problem. A policy trying to reach the fully efficient interior combination may need one instrument for the activity and another for informative testing. An activity-only payment cannot generally correct both choices under the specified conditions. This is a conditional result about incentives, not a universal demand for two new taxes.

For the fictional council, the distinction is practical. A charge on Aster's restrictive condition could encourage a more open arrangement. A contract paying for independently verified tests could reward useful evidence directly. Either could affect the other. If the condition changes, fewer purchased tests may be needed. If good substitute tests become cheap, preserving the old route may become less valuable.

The relevant activity is therefore not automatically electricity use, model size, or profit. The draft permits a declared productive or restrictive choice whose effects can be specified. An actual tax authority would need an administrable way to observe that choice and the informative testing margin. These are demanding requirements, not details to leave until after announcing a rate.

Consider the computers running Aster's service. Some produce ordinary answers. Others test alternative models, investigate failures, or compare designs. A tax on all computing would reach both production and evaluation. If the proposed correction is meant to discourage lost comparison while encouraging useful tests, this single base could combine margins requiring different treatment.

That observation does not establish that computing should never be taxed. Electricity, emissions, congestion, revenue needs, and distributional objectives can supply other questions. It establishes that a tax justified by lost information needs evidence connecting its chosen base to that loss. Calling a familiar base convenient does not supply the connection.

Profit has a similar limitation. Aster's high profit could reflect a useful service, a fortunate history, a restrictive condition, or a combination. It is not a meter of the particular external cost. Corporate income taxation can have other rationales and effects; the comparison argument does not replace the analysis of those systems with one universal surcharge on successful firms.

The Swedish system makes the contrast memorable. Its charged emissions and credited useful production were separately specified. An informational instrument would need its own defensible distinctions. It cannot achieve them by renaming every computer operation pollution or every unpopular profit an information loss.

## When public tests replace private ones

Now let the government finance a testing program. It announces that an additional set of evaluations will be available to the relevant decision maker. The program might be useful. Its announced test count does not tell us how much new evidence will exist after private actors respond.

In the draft's substitutable-trial setting, Aster has a desired total level of evidence at a given activity. If a public test supplies evidence Aster would otherwise have purchased, the firm can reduce its own testing. While that replacement continues, total information can remain unchanged. Government has bought tests without increasing the relevant total.[^chapter_11-c11-8]

Imagine the council funding a set of checked maintenance requests. Aster had planned to pay an evaluator for equivalent work and cancels part of that commission when the public results become available. Counting only the council's new evaluations would overstate the additional evidence. The displaced private work belongs in the comparison.

This need not make the program wasteful. If the public evaluator supplies equivalent evidence at lower real cost, society can save resources while preserving the same information. If the public route is more expensive, or costly to establish, replacing private provision may be a poor bargain. The question is total evidence and total cost after the response.

The exact one-for-one crowdout result depends on strong assumptions: interchangeable informative tests, known quality, the stated private objective, and the absence of binding capacity constraints. Different public measurements may reveal something private tests do not. A firm may also lack the ability to buy more. These differences can change the result, and an application must investigate them.

California's Electric Program Investment Charge provides a real example of a different financing route. Utility customers fund research administered by the Energy Commission and participating utilities. The program supports research, development, and demonstration directed toward electricity-system goals. Its existence shows an institutional way to pay for shared investigation; it does not by itself prove that each funded project adds knowledge beyond private spending.[^chapter_11-c11-9]

On December 20, 2022, an earthquake struck Ferndale, California, causing a widespread power outage lasting nearly sixteen hours. According to the project team's January 2026 report, the Redwood Coast Airport Microgrid provided backup power for nearly fifteen hours. Its customers included the airport and a Coast Guard air station. Solar generation and battery storage had acquired a practical test.[^chapter_11-c11-10]

The installation also had a persistent fault. Converters linking the large solar array to the batteries repeatedly triggered protective trips. The report identified an unwanted current path to ground, especially in humid conditions. After repeated attempts at repair, replacement equipment was installed and tested. The fault impaired solar generation while another part of the system still allowed backup operation.[^chapter_11-c11-10]

That combination matters. A project can deliver useful service, incur a costly failure, and produce a lesson for someone else. Another community might avoid the converter problem without ever buying electricity from this installation. Whether that lesson changes its choice, and what avoiding the mistake is worth, would need investigation. We have a possible beneficiary and a consequential decision, rather than an automatic social return on every research dollar.

The same report identifies a difficult business case. Readily monetizable benefits did not cover the assessed costs, while the value of resilience depended on the services and community involved. This is a project-team assessment, with its own assumptions, rather than proof that a subsidy of any particular size was socially optimal.

The distinction matters. A demonstration can produce electricity, resilience, operating experience, and information for other projects. Those benefits may have different recipients. A commercial revenue shortfall does not measure all the wider value, but neither does the phrase public benefit establish that wider value exceeds the cost.

Imagine the converter diagnosis remaining in a private maintenance log. It could improve the owner's engineering while never reaching another community's purchasing decision. Our tax draft assumes delivery to a common decision maker; this imagined arrangement breaks that premise. The private work still counts as evidence for the decision it informs. Its existence would not establish that the outside community received an adequate substitute. Sharing findings and generating new tests address different gaps.

For an AI evaluation program, the analogous questions are concrete. Which future buyers use the results? Do they change procurement? Does the program reveal failures that internal tests missed? Does a supplier reduce its own work in response? A publicly available benchmark that nobody uses may have less value than a small, credible test that changes an important contract.

The Redwood report's attention to lessons and remaining replication barriers is useful in this respect. Publishing a result does not make another installation straightforward. Likewise, releasing an AI test does not ensure that it answers another buyer's problem. Transporting knowledge requires relevant similarities, usable documentation, and a realistic account of what remains different.

William Kerr, Ramana Nanda, and Matthew Rhodes-Kropf describe entrepreneurship as experimentation, including the value of learning before committing to the full cost of a venture. That established perspective helps clarify what funded evaluation should buy: evidence capable of improving a continuation decision. A project that reveals a reason to stop can produce useful knowledge even without creating a successful business.[^chapter_11-c11-11]

But failure is not automatically proof of a valuable experiment. A poorly specified test can spend money and leave the decision unresolved. Paying for informative evaluation requires identifying what was learned, how credibly, and for whom. The possibility of useful failure should protect honest experimentation, not excuse a program from having an evidentiary purpose.

## What replacement can change

Return to the council's invoice. If a lost independent trial can be replaced by an equally informative test at a known feasible cost, the economic sacrifice need not equal the full decision loss that would occur without any replacement. The buyer can mitigate the harm. The cost of doing so matters.[^chapter_11-c11-12]

The tax draft formalizes this point within its substitutable-trial experiment. Once replacement is worthwhile and available, the marginal cost of losing an organic trial is limited by the cost of replacing it. Preserving every original channel at any price would ignore the substitute. This limit depends on the substitute actually working.

For the fictional maintenance service, a set of independently checked requests might provide enough evidence for next year's contract. Buying that evaluation could be cheaper than maintaining two complete operating systems indefinitely. If so, the best response to the lost route might be replacement testing, alongside whatever terms make its results usable.

In another case the proposed replacement might fail. A small laboratory test could omit how staff learn, how suppliers improve after receiving work, or how residents change their behavior. If those responses determine the future contract's performance, copying a few requests into a laboratory may not supply the relevant evidence. A cheap test of a different question is not a cheap substitute.

The difference connects back to shadow futures. Replaying a modeled allocation process holds its underlying effects, starting conditions, and response rules fixed. Creating a new public laboratory changes an institution. Its observations may help estimate a relevant effect, but equivalence to the lost comparison requires an argument. We should not assume it merely because both activities are called tests.

Replacement also has fixed costs. An evaluator may need secure infrastructure, qualified staff, procedures, and independent checks before the first useful result appears. A lower cost per test need not make a new facility worthwhile for a small program. Enough useful work must justify establishing it.

One way to reduce that burden would be to use an existing qualified evaluator. Another would be to share infrastructure across buyers while preserving the relevant independence of their tests. But combining everything in one facility could create common measurement errors or a new bottleneck. The least expensive institution on paper may be costly if its conclusions cannot be trusted or challenged. Before financing a permanent laboratory, the council could commission a limited piece of work to learn whether the route functions. That preliminary contract is itself an experiment in institutional design. Its result should help decide whether a larger commitment is worthwhile, rather than make the larger commitment inevitable because the facility has acquired a constituency.

The microgrid report offers a concrete reminder that technical success and scalable institutional feasibility are different questions. The team described remaining costs and complexities even after demonstrating a functioning system. An AI laboratory similarly needs a business or public-financing arrangement that can sustain useful work. A promising demonstration is evidence to examine, not a completed case for permanent provision.

A sensible appraisal compares whole routes. Keep the original opportunity, buy private replacements, establish public provision, or combine narrower measures. The choice should account for how production and testing change together. If a cheaper replacement makes integration less costly to society, the efficient response could permit more productive activity while preserving useful evidence.

That possibility is important to the book's constructive argument. Taking counterfactual economies seriously does not mean freezing the existing organization of every market. It means considering feasible alternatives and the information needed to choose among them. Sometimes a new institution makes it possible to capture scale benefits without losing the consequential comparison.

## Who pays, and for what?

Britain's retail-banking reforms reveal a financing choice that is easy to miss when focusing on interfaces. The 2017 order required providers to supply resources for the implementation arrangements. It also required providers to fund independent service-quality surveys, with contributions linked to active accounts in the relevant survey market. Comparison infrastructure had an explicit payer.[^chapter_11-c11-13]

The account-based survey allocation also makes the purpose of a proxy easier to see. Organizers need a workable way to divide the bill among participating providers. They can choose a transparent rule and enforce it. That is different from estimating how much each provider's conduct reduced society's future ability to learn. A larger bank could owe a larger contribution under the funding convention even if its own testing and data practices were excellent. A smaller bank could have poor practices while owing less. Neither possibility makes the cost-sharing rule incoherent. It means that we should evaluate it as a way to finance a defined service, with its own distributional and incentive effects, rather than describe it as a completed measurement of informational harm.

This is not an example of my proposed tax being adopted. It is a regulatory funding arrangement for specified duties. Its relevance is narrower: a policy can require access, require evidence, and allocate the cost of producing that evidence. These are related decisions, not one automatic consequence of a declaration that competition is desirable.

The distinction applies to our fictional council. It could purchase an evaluation from general funds, require suppliers to contribute under lawful contract terms, or consider a payment tied to a demonstrated restrictive action. Each choice answers a different financing or incentive question. An instrument can raise enough money for useful work while still doing little to discourage the behavior that removed a comparison.

There are also two different entries in the public account. Aster's payment can become revenue available to finance useful work. The evaluator's staff time, secure computing, and supervision are resources consumed in doing that work. Adding the tax payment to the testing bill and calling both destroyed output would count a transfer as if it were a second resource cost. Ignoring the staff time because a tax paid for it would make the opposite mistake. Who pays remains important for distribution and behavior, while what gets used determines the resource burden. A transparent appraisal should show both. If charging the payment also alters prices, entry, or investment, those additional consequences need their own analysis; they are not automatically equal to the amount collected. Suppose a fee on every AI response pays for an excellent testing service. The service might deserve support. The fee could nevertheless discourage useful production or useful evaluation in ways unrelated to the particular information loss. Its funding rationale and its corrective rationale should be assessed separately.

The formal payer may also differ from the person who ultimately bears the burden. A supplier might adjust prices, wages, investment, or contract terms. The possibilities depend on the market and the instrument. We cannot call a payment a charge on Aster and assume its owners alone will bear every consequence.

Public money has an opportunity cost too. Funding a laboratory can displace another program, require revenue, or interact with existing taxes. The draft's clean corrective benchmark treats transfers simply and holds the operating firm fixed. Real financing, entry, avoidance, and jurisdictional responses require a wider analysis. The benchmark is useful precisely because its limits are stated.

Rothschild and Scheuer's work supplies a relevant prior warning: changing an income tax can alter the relative returns to productive and rent-seeking activities. The indirect response can weaken or strengthen the direct correction. We should bring that habit of reasoning to an informational instrument rather than stop after naming an externality.

Suppose a rule rewards every recorded supplier exposure. Firms could satisfy it with repetitive easy cases that reveal little. Suppose it pays for benchmark improvements. Developers could concentrate on the benchmark. A useful evidence-purchase contract needs enforceable quality, relevant tasks, and credible delivery. Administratively countable activity is not always the economic object we want.

Aster could run the same easy request repeatedly and produce an enormous test count. Birch could present only the requests on which it looks strongest. Both examples would satisfy a careless payment rule more cheaply than a genuinely useful comparison. The remedy is to define and verify the evidence purchased: task selection, relevant variation, checked outcomes, version records, and delivery to the decision maker. Some of this can be specified contractually. Some may require independent review. Those enforcement costs reduce the net benefit of paying for tests, and should be included before the program is described as a cheap substitute for access.

The Swedish emissions system again makes the measurement issue visible. Its agency describes comparing facility measurements with independent accredited measurements. The details are specific to emissions, but the institutional requirement travels: a payment tied to a reported quantity needs a way to check that quantity. For informative testing, verification must concern the test's meaning as well as whether it occurred.

## Before choosing a rate

The strongest proposed version of the argument has a demanding chain. Identify the action. Establish its effect on useful evidence. Identify the consequential decision and the beneficiaries. Estimate or bound the loss after private responses and feasible replacement. Compare instruments with their implementation, financing, and productive effects. Then ask whether a corrective payment improves the attainable outcome.

Our council's invoice can help with one link. It may reveal the cost of a particular replacement evaluation. It cannot establish every link in that chain. The authority still needs to know whether the evaluation substitutes for the lost opportunity, whether its result changes the decision, and which benefits Aster already had reason to protect.
